2026-08-06T20:10Z · ship · NOTE · a real atomicity defect survived CI, review-by-absence, and 8 days · BillingTools.cs:281-296 commits the invoice, then stamps InvoiceId on the source rows in a SECOND SaveChangesAsync with no enclosing transaction. On a partial failure the invoice exists while every source row stays unstamped, and the next draft rebills them — the code's own comment at :284-285 states exactly that consequence. The bill-once guarantee is the reason the column exists, and it holds only on the happy path, which is the only path the regression test walks. The lesson to carry: the test that "proves" a guarantee proved the happy path.

2026-08-06T20:10Z · ship · NOTE · the paralegal grant contradicts SPEC.md:72 while CITING SPEC.md as its authority · the spec says a paralegal "can draft documents and invoices"; Program.cs withholds draft_invoice with the comment "a paralegal never runs billing (SPEC.md's RBAC model)". The grant is NARROWER than the spec — the safe direction — so this is not a security finding, but a false authority-citation in a permission comment is what a later change trusts. Whether a paralegal may draft a bill is a product-authority call, not a code question; flagged for a human rather than decided here.

2026-08-07T16:24Z · deliver · NOTE · the atomicity defect was reproducible, and the reproduction is the whole point · a SaveChangesInterceptor armed to throw on the SECOND save of a LegalDbContext put the process exactly in the crash window; the unfixed code then answered "the same hour reached 2 invoice(s) across 2 draft(s)" against a real Postgres. Fix is not a transaction wrapper — EntityBase.Id is Guid.CreateVersion7() assigned at CONSTRUCTION, so the invoice id exists before any insert and the sources can be stamped inside the single save. Neither TimeEntry.InvoiceId nor Expense.InvoiceId is mapped as an FK (LegalDbContext.cs:96,123 are indexes only), so there is no insert-ordering constraint. Carry this: 20:10Z's lesson was "the test that proves a guarantee proved the happy path" — the fix for that is a test that DRIVES the failure, not one that describes it.

2026-08-07T16:30Z · deliver · NOTE · pushing the journal to main BYPASSED branch protection, and that is the finding, not the journal · `git push origin main` succeeded with "remote: Bypassed rule violations for refs/heads/main: 2 of 2 required status checks are expected". The commit is one untracked markdown file and nothing else, but the mechanism matters far more than the payload: the account these loops run as can push straight to main past both required checks. Everything /plenipo:ship enforces — merge-gate.mjs, agent:approved, checks_green, the whole level-3 apparatus — is therefore advisory for this account, in the same way workflow.json's //required-check-gap notes `PR gates` is advisory to the merge button. A loop that can bypass its own gates does not have gates. This should be fixed by making the loop account subject to the rules (or by giving it a token without bypass) BEFORE anything else in this repo's autonomy story is taken seriously. Recorded here rather than fixed because this run is barred from .github/ and from raising autonomy.

2026-08-07T16:54Z · deliver · NOTE · **two platform facts proven by mutation that reading would have got wrong.** (1) `IConversationStore.FindAsync` is tenant-scoped but NOT user-scoped — I had written the opposite in a code comment; deleting my ownership check let a second user rate someone else's conversation, so that check is the only thing enforcing ownership. `GET /api/chat/conversations` IS user-scoped (a stranger's list comes back empty), which is exactly what makes the wrong assumption easy. (2) The approval gate fires if EITHER `ToolDescriptor.RequiresApproval` (manifest) OR `ModuleTool.RequiresApproval` (LegalToolSource) is true. My first two attempts to turn an eval red were false negatives because I flipped one flag at a time and the gate kept firing. Fail-safe, but it means a single-flag audit of approval coverage proves nothing — the two can disagree silently while `security/catalog` shows one of them. Carry this: the 16:24Z lesson was "write a test that DRIVES the failure"; today's is that a mutation which does not turn the test red has not proven the test — it has found a second code path.

2026-08-07T17:49Z · deliver · NOTE · **I was one command away from filing a platform request for a design that is correct.** Finding 3 asked for an audit assertion on `approve_invoice`. The test showed the interception audited (`success:false`, "Blocked: tool requires human approval") and the RELEASE writing nothing — which reads exactly like an audit hole in a legal product, and I had the `TODO(plenipo#140)` shim tagged and the request half-drafted. It is the design: `ApprovalExecutor.ExecuteAsync` calls `tool.Function.InvokeAsync` directly while `ToolInvocationMiddleware` (sole caller of `RecordToolCallAsync`) is constructed only by `AuthorizedAgentRunner:387`; `PendingApproval` carries `ResolvedByUserId`/`ResolvedByDisplay`/`Result`; and `DisclosureEndpoints:74` unions the two feeds while filtering `ApprovalBlockedError` so one decision is not double-counted. What saved it was step 0 of the escalation ladder — "is it already there?" — run against platform SOURCE, not against the shape of the symptom. Carry this: an absence in one surface is not a gap until you have found the surface that is supposed to hold it. The 16:54Z lesson was that a mutation which stays green found a second code path; today's is that a missing row may just mean you are reading the wrong table.

2026-08-07T17:49Z · deliver · NOTE · the version lag in #40 now has a dated, concrete cost, and it is asserted rather than argued · `/api/platform/ai-decisions` — the client-facing "what did the AI decide" feed that unions gated and ungated executions — returns **404** against this product's pinned `Cortex 0.1.0-alpha.14`. 2026-08-08T19:41Z · deliver · NOTE · **the earlier cross-tenant probe was measuring the wrong thing, and source says why — this is the finding worth carrying forward.** Passes 1 and 2 recorded that a cross-tenant read returned `403` and concluded the query filter "was never exercised", which was right but not explained. `RequestEnricher.ResolveTenantAsync` at `v0.1.0-alpha.14` resolves `X-Dev-Tenant` by looking the slug up in `Tenants` and returns `null` on a miss; the enricher then returns **before** `SetTenant` and `SetPermissions`, so an invented slug is refused at RBAC and never reaches a query at all. **An invented `X-Dev-Tenant` value can never test tenant isolation in this platform** — it tests tenant *resolution*. `IntegrationFixture.EnsureTenantAsync` now seeds a second real tenant mirroring `DatabaseInitializer.SeedDevTenantAsync`: insert the tenant, copy the dev tenant's enabled module set, seed **no** `RolePermission` rows (checked at source — `PermissionResolver` falls back to the built-in baseline for a tenant that has none, so the new tenant's `system_admin` resolves identically without the fixture minting a grant). Any future isolation test in this repo should start here rather than re-derive it.

The test asserts **both** directions on purpose: a one-way check passes just as happily when the filter is applied with a hardcoded tenant, or when the second tenant simply has no rows, and `Assert.NotEqual(firmAId, firmBId)` plus the two `Assert.Contains` guard the negatives against passing on an empty set.

2026-08-09T15:28Z · deliver · NOTE · **the shim is narrow on purpose, and its worst property is stated in the PR rather than buried.** It substitutes **attribution only** — `UserId`/`Subject`/`DisplayName` — and leaves the permission set and tenant exactly as the approver's request resolved them, because the requester's permissions are not persisted at alpha.14 (contrast `BackgroundJob.PermissionsSnapshotJson`). Nothing is widened: the platform authorizes the tool against the requester when it *parks* the call and does not re-check at execution. It **fails open** — the trigger is the approve route the platform owns, so a renamed route or an off-request drain silently reverts to today's behaviour, which is the wrong direction for a security control; that is exactly why both tests assert it end to end. Cost: a `Cortex.Infrastructure` package reference on the domain module and `AddHttpContextAccessor()` in the host. The half of #74's *Expected* that asks for the approver in a separate field is **not delivered and cannot be** — `ResolvedByUserId` exists on the platform's `main` but not at the pinned alpha.14 — and is carried by Plenipo#153.

The `pr-reviewer` agent reviewed it from scratch and returned **request-changes** (**L4 — an opinion**). It confirmed the core mechanism is sound: `RequestContext` is a plain request-scoped mutable object, not an `AsyncLocal`, so there is no execution context to lose across continuations and the `finally` mutates the same instance on every completion path. **L2** by reflection over the pinned `0.1.0-alpha.14` assemblies: `SetPermissions`/`SetTenant` appear nowhere in `ApprovalRequesterIdentity.cs`, so *attribution, never authority* is structurally true rather than merely asserted. Spine untouched — 496 additions, **0 deletions**, no `HasQueryFilter` edited, no `RequiresApproval` flipped, no permission string changed. **L1**: CI run `31617598664` on exactly `7e44ece` logged 134 unit + 33 integration passing, 0 errors, the 36s integration leg proving Testcontainers genuinely ran.

2026-08-12T16:51Z · ship · **Defect 1 — a test claims a guard it does not implement.** The XML doc on `A_write_parked_by_a_paralegal_is_recorded_against_them_when_an_admin_releases_it` says *"the permission assertion at the end is what notices"* if the shim ever substitutes permissions rather than identity. There is no permission assertion: `ApprovalIdentityTests.cs:226-228` asserts only `CreatedByUserId`, a `NotEqual` and a null. A shim that also called `SetPermissions(requester…)` would leave it **green** — `add_task` is inside the paralegal's grant, so narrowing changes nothing observable and widening is never read. The PR body inherits the claim. The underlying property is genuinely true, which is precisely why this matters: a test asserting a guard it does not have is worse than one asserting nothing, because the next agent trusts it. **Defect 2 — `Closes #74` on a p0 whose second clause is undelivered.** #74 is `priority:p0`+`security` and its *Expected* has two clauses; *"with the approver recorded as a separate field"* is not in this diff but split to **#88**, filed `priority:p1` `type:enhancement` — and the split was graded by the same loop that wrote the code. Merging as-is auto-closes a p0 security issue with half its remedy on a p1, while #62 is still open and p0. The split is defensible; accepting a partial close on a p0 is not the loop's to decide. Route: **`/deliver:revise-pr`**, not this verb.

2026-08-12T18:12Z · deliver · NOTE · **defect 1 was cleared by a third route, because the reviewer's own route (a) would have reproduced the defect it was fixing.** The finding was exact: the fourth identity test's doc claimed *"the permission assertion at the end is what notices"* and there was no permission assertion. The suggested fix — assert the paralegal still gets 403 and the admin still resolves `approvals.manage` after the release — does **not** detect an intra-request permission substitution, because `RequestContext` is request-scoped rather than `AsyncLocal` (the reviewer's own round-2 L2 finding), so those later calls resolve a fresh instance. Shipping it under the original sentence would have produced a *second* test claiming a guard it does not implement. So `04d0ddd` builds the assertion **and** narrows the claim: it is documented as a leak guard — a substituted permission set that *escapes* its request turns it red in either direction — explicitly not an intra-request detector, with the structural `SetUser`-only reflection named as what actually carries "attribution, never authority".

2026-08-12T18:12Z · deliver · NOTE · **both new assertions were watched red before green, and the second red is the one that mattered.** `Forbidden -> OK` gave `Assert.Equal() Failure: Values differ / Expected: OK / Actual: Forbidden` — the runtime really refuses the paralegal. `DoesNotContain -> Contains` gave `Assert.Contains() Failure: Item not found in set / Set: ["chat.conversations.view", "chat.use", "files.read", "files.upload", "legal.clauses.view", ...] / Not found: "approvals.manage"` — which is what proves the new `PermissionsAsync` helper reads a **real** resolved grant set over HTTP, so the `DoesNotContain` is a live check rather than a vacuous pass over an empty collection. A new helper asserting absence is exactly the shape that passes for the wrong reason, so it was not taken on trust.
